}+\ n

contradicting boundedness.
∎

Let us now see how these categories correspond precisely to theories satisfying our principles. We call a quantum category
non-trivial
when it has
\mathrm{id}_{I}\neq 0
.

Theorem 7.9
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between non-trivial:

•

quantum categories
\mathbf{A}
;

•

dagger theories
\mathbf{C}
satisfying the operational principles;

up to equivalence, via
\mathbf{A}=\mathsf{GP}({\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{pure}}})
and
\mathbf{C}=\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

For any such
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{A}=\mathsf{GP}({\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{pure}}})
is a quantum category by Proposition
7.2
, and must be non-trivial since otherwise
\mathrm{id}_{I}=0
in
\mathbf{C}
, making every object there trivial. By Lemma
3.2
the functor
[-]
extends to an equivalence of dagger theories
\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})\simeq\mathbf{C}
.

The converse direction requires that for any non-trivial quantum category
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
satisfies the operational principles and is equivalent to
\mathsf{GP}({\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})_{\mathsf{pure}}})
. This fact is not needed for our main reconstruction, but is proven in Proposition
A.2
in Appendix
A
.
∎

This is a strong result, since for general
\mathbf{C}
with an environment structure there may be many
\mathbf{A}
with
\mathbf{C}\simeq\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
.

Example 7.10
.

If
S
is a phased ring which is also a field,
\mathbf{Quant}_{S}
satisfies our principles. In particular so do
\mathbf{Quant}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathbf{Quant}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Remark 7.11
.

In this section we focused on the category
\mathsf{GP}({\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{pure}}})
, but
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{pure}}
may also be axiomatised similarly; see Appendix
D
.

8.
Reconstructions

We now return to the setting of dagger theories
\mathbf{C}
satisfying our principles, and have reached our main result.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a dagger theory satisfying the operational principles and
R=\mathbf{C}(I,I)
. There is an embedding of dagger theories

\mathbf{Quant}_{S}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{C}

(17)

which preserves coarse-graining, for some phased ring
S
with
R\simeq S^{\mathrm{pos}}
as semi-rings. Moreover if
R
is bounded this is an equivalence of theories
\mathbf{C}\simeq\mathbf{Quant}_{S}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.9
there is an equivalence of dagger theories
\mathbf{C}\simeq\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
where
\mathbf{A}
is a quantum category. Moreover, note that any such equivalence must preserve the coarse-graining operation, since it is unique by Proposition
5.7
. Hence it suffices to assume that
\mathbf{C}
is of this form, in which case .
S=\mathbf{A}(I,I)
is indeed a phased ring. By dagger normalisation in
\mathbf{A}
the scalars in
\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
are isomorphic to
S^{\mathrm{pos}}
, since for every state
\psi
in
\mathbf{A}
we have

\psi
\psi
=
\psi
\psi
=
\phi
\phi
\ s\
s^{\dagger}\!
=
\ s\
s^{\dagger}\!

for some isometric state
\phi
and scalar
s
in
\mathbf{A}
.

The embedding
\mathsf{Mat}_{S}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{A}
is an equivalence when
R
is bounded thanks to Lemma
7.8
, and it induces the respective embedding or equivalence (
17
). Since the former preserves biproducts, the latter preserves coarse-graining.
∎

As a result, when
\mathbf{C}
satisfies the operational principles, its scalars
R=\mathbf{C}(I,I)
form the positive elements
S^{\mathrm{pos}}
of a phased ring
S
, generalising the relationship between
\mathbb{R^{+}}
and
\mathbb{C}
. This provides
R
with nice properties; it has characteristic
0
and that
a
is divisible by
a+b
for all
a,b
, hence coming with an embedding
\mathbb{Q}^{+}\hookrightarrow R
.

We may also freely extend
R
to a ring
D(R)
, its
difference ring
. Formally
D(R)
consists of pairs
(a,b)
of elements of
R
after identifying
(a,b)
with
(c,d)
whenever
a+d=b+c
. Addition and multiplication are defined in the obvious way when interpreting
(a,b)
as ‘
a-b
’. For example
D(\mathbb{R}^{+})=\mathbb{R}
.

Under a final extra condition we can show that
S
resembles either the real or complex numbers. Say that a semi-ring
R
has
square roots
when every
a\in R
has
a=b^{2}
for some
b\in R
. For any ring
S
we write
S[i]
for the involutive ring with elements of the form
a+b\cdot i
for
a,b\in S
, where
1=-i^{2}=i\cdot i^{\dagger}
, and we define
a^{\dagger}=a
for all
a\in S
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
S
be a phased ring for which
R=S^{\mathrm{pos}}
has square roots.

1.

Every non-zero
s\in S
has
s=r\cdot u
for a unique
r\in R
and unitary
u\in S
.

2.

R
is totally ordered under
a\leq b
whenever
a+c=b
for some
c\in R
.

3.

D(R)\simeq S^{\mathsf{sa}}:=\{s\in S\mid s^{\dagger}=s\}
.

4.

Either
S=S^{\mathsf{sa}}
with trivial involution, or
S
has square roots and
S=S^{\mathsf{sa}}[i]
.

Proof.

1
.
For uniqueness, suppose that
p\cdot u=l\cdot v
for
p,l\in R
and
u,v
unitary. Then
p=l\cdot w
where
w=v\cdot u^{-1}
is unitary. So

l\cdot w^{\dagger}=p^{\dagger}=p=l\cdot w

Since
S
is an integral domain, multiplication is cancellative so
w=w^{\dagger}
and
w^{2}=1
. If
w=1
we are done, otherwise
w=-1
and so
p+l=0
. But then
p=l=0
by the definition of a phased ring.
For existence, given
s\in S
let
r=s^{\dagger}\cdot s\in S^{\mathrm{pos}}
. Since
S^{\mathrm{pos}}
has square roots,
r=t^{2}
for some
t\in S^{\mathrm{pos}}
. Then
s^{\dagger}\cdot s=t^{\dagger}\cdot t
, so
s=t\cdot u
for some
u
, which is easily seen to be unitary.

2
. Let
s\in S^{\mathsf{sa}}
be non-zero with
s=t\cdot u
as above. Then
t\cdot u=t\cdot u^{\dagger}
and so
u=u^{\dagger}
giving
u=\pm 1
. Hence either
s\in R
or
-s\in R
. Then
\forall a,b\in R
either

a-b\in R\quad\text{ or }\quad b-a\in R

making
R
totally ordered in the above manner.

3
. We may identify
D(R)
with the set of elements
a-b\in S
for
a,b\in R
. Then
D(R)\subseteq S^{\mathsf{sa}}
always, but by the previous part
S^{\mathsf{sa}}\subseteq D(R)
.

4
. Suppose that
S\neq S^{\mathsf{sa}}
. We will show that
S
has square roots using techniques adapted from Vicary
[
Vic11
, Thm. 4.2]
. Since
R
has square roots, thanks to the first part it suffices to find a square root of any given unitary
u\in S
. For this, first suppose that for all
s\in S
the element

x_{s}:=s+u\cdot s^{\dagger}

is zero. Then putting
s=1
shows that
u=-1
. But then since
x_{s}=0
for all
s
we have
S=S^{\mathsf{sa}}
, a contradiction. Hence there is
s\in S
such that
x_{s}
is non-zero. Letting
x_{s}=r\cdot v
for a unitary
v
and
r\in R
we have

r\cdot v^{\dagger}=x_{s}^{\dagger}=u\cdot x_{s}=r\cdot v\cdot u^{\dagger}

so that
v^{\dagger}=v\cdot u^{\dagger}
and
v^{2}=u
as desired.

Now in particular,
-1
has a unitary square root
i
. Finally note that
2
is divisible in
R
thanks to the embedding
\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}\hookrightarrow R
. Then for any
s\in S
defining elements of
S^{\mathsf{sa}}
by

\mathsf{R}(s):=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(s+s^{\dagger})\qquad\mathsf{I}(s):=\frac{i}{2}(s^{\dagger}-s)

we have
s=\mathsf{R}(s)+i\cdot\mathsf{I}(s)
so that
S=S^{\mathsf{sa}}[i]
.
∎

This gives a more precise form of our reconstruction theorem.

Corollary 8.3
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a dagger theory satisfying the operational principles, and suppose that
R=\mathbf{C}(I,I)
has square roots and is bounded. Then
\mathbf{C}
is equivalent to

\mathbf{Quant}_{D(R)}\quad\text{ or }\quad\mathbf{Quant}_{D(R)[i]}

9.
Probabilistic Theories

Let us now consider the typical physical setting in which scalars correspond to (unnormalised) probabilities.

Definition 9.1
.

We call a dagger theory with coarse-graining
\mathbf{C}

probabilistic
when it comes with an isomorphism of semi-rings
\mathbf{C}(I,I)\simeq\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Note that this is a weaker definition than typical in the literature
[
Bar07
,
CDP11
]
since we have not made any assumptions relating to tomography, finite-dimensionality or topological closure. One may in fact identify such theories intrinsically, as in the following observation for which we thank John van de Wetering.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a dagger theory satisfying the operational principles. Then
\mathbf{C}
is probabilistic iff
R=\mathbf{C}(I,I)
has square roots, no infinitesimals, and that every bounded increasing sequence has a supremum.

Proof.

Appendix
A
.
∎

Now immediately from the definition of a probabilistic theory, and Corollary
8.3
, we have the following.

Corollary 9.3
.

Any dagger theory which satisfies the operational principles and is probabilistic is equivalent to
\mathbf{Quant}_{\mathbb{R}}
or
\mathbf{Quant}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Finally one may distinguish complex from real quantum theory by adding an extra principle such as
local tomography

[
HW12
]
, or that every phase of a phased biproduct in our pure subcategory has a square root. In future it would be desirable to find a more generic categorical property separating these theories.

10.
Discussion

We close with some points of discussion.

Equivalent axioms

We saw that our principles ensured the presence of a coarse-graining operation
+
on morphisms. Surprisingly, this operation is in fact cancellative, ruling out ‘possibilistic’ theories like
\mathbf{Rel}
, as we show in Appendix
C
. Taking this operation instead as a primitive yields an alternative formulation of our principles, as follows.

Firstly, in a theory with coarse-graining, let us call a morphism
f

sub-causal
when

.

(18)

Proof.

Appendix
C
.
∎

Note that condition
2
is certainly satisfied in any probabilistic theory. Hence any such theory satisfying the remaining conditions is equivalent to
\mathbf{Quant}_{\mathbb{R}}
or
\mathbf{Quant}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Phased rings and fields

A basic open question left from Section
7
is the following: is every phased ring
S
a field? Indeed in any such
S
every element of the form
1+s^{\dagger}\cdot s
is invertible, our motivating examples
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
are indeed fields, and it is only in this case that we showed that
\mathbf{Quant}_{S}
satisfies our assumptions (Example
7.7
). It is easy to see that when all non-zero scalars in our theory
\mathbf{C}
are invertible (as in a probabilistic theory) the phased ring
S
of scalars in
\mathsf{GP}({\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{pure}}})
will be a field; for more on this see Appendix
E
.

Daggers

Our reconstruction made extensive use of the dagger, which lacks a clear physical interpretation for non-pure processes. It would thus be desirable to avoid explicit use of the dagger, or instead derive its presence as well as dagger compactness from principles such as purification as is essentially done in
[
CDP11
]
. This would yield a fully operational reconstruction in the simple framework of symmetric monoidal categories with discarding. The removal of dagger compactness would also allow infinite-dimensional systems to be considered.
Finally, weakening our principles to allow for super-selection rules would allow us to study the broader category of finite-dimensional C*-algebras and completely positive maps
[
CHK14
]
.

Appendix A
Omitted Proofs

Proof of Lemma
6.4
.

This is essentially
[
Tul18b
, Lemma 7.2]
. Consider such an object
I\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}I
, say with coprojections
|0\rangle,|1\rangle
. For any object
A
, we claim that the object
A\otimes(I\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}I)
forms a phased dagger biproduct
A\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}A
with coprojections

A
A
:=
\ \kappa_{i}\
A
i
I\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}I
I\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}I
A

for
i=0,1
. First, note that for any morphism
f
into another object
B
we have

\iff
f

f

A
B
A
B
i
=
f_{i}
A
B
=
i
f_{i}
A
B

for
i=0,1
. From this it is straightforward to see that
A\otimes(I\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}I)
satisfies the first condition of a phased biproduct, with induced morphisms being unique up to a phase, and that every phase is of the form
\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes u
for some phase
u
of
I\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}I
. This makes phases closed under the dagger, so that
I\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}I
is a phased dagger biproduct as required.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.5
.

The condition is necessary since for any phased dagger biproduct
A\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}B
it may be seen that
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{B}
are dagger kernels with
\kappa_{A}={\kappa_{B}}^{\bot}
.

Conversely, suppose that the condition holds and let
k\colon A\to C
and
l\colon B\to C
be orthogonal kernels.
Let
f
be any endomorphism of
C\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}C
with
f\circ\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{1}\circ k\circ k^{\dagger}
and
f\circ\kappa_{2}=\kappa_{2}\circ l\circ l^{\dagger}
and let
i:=\mathrm{im}(f)
. Then since

\mathrm{coker}(f)\circ\kappa_{1}\circ k=\mathrm{coker}(f)\circ f\circ\kappa_{1}\circ k=0

and similarly for
\kappa_{2}
and
l
, there are unique
\kappa_{A},\kappa_{B}
making the following commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Im}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

We claim that
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{B}
make
\mathrm{Im}(f)
a phased biproduct
A\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}B
. To see the existence property, given
g\colon A\to D
and
h\colon B\to D
, let
j\colon C\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}C\to D
with
j\circ\kappa_{1}=g\circ k^{\dagger}
and
j\circ\kappa_{2}=h\circ l^{\dagger}
. Then the morphism
a:=j\circ i
has
a\circ\kappa_{A}=g
and
a\circ\kappa_{B}=h
.

We now show the uniqueness property. First, it is straightforward to show that
f^{\dagger}
has the same composites with
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
as
f
. Then
f^{\dagger}\circ\kappa_{1}\circ k^{\bot}=0
and so since
i=\mathrm{im}(f)
we have
i^{\dagger}\circ\kappa_{1}\circ k^{\bot}=0
. Then since
k=\mathrm{im}(k)
we have

i^{\dagger}\circ\kappa_{1}=i^{\dagger}\circ\kappa_{1}\circ k\circ k^{\dagger}=i^{\dagger}\circ i\circ\kappa_{A}\circ k^{\dagger}=\kappa_{A}\circ k^{\dagger}

Now suppose that some morphisms
m,p\colon\mathrm{Im}(f)\to D
each have the same composites with
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{B}
. Let
q=m\circ i^{\dagger}
and
r=p\circ i^{\dagger}
. Then

\displaystyle q\circ\kappa_{1}=m\circ i^{\dagger}\circ\kappa_{1}=m\circ\kappa_{A}\circ k^{\dagger}=p\circ\kappa_{A}\circ k^{\dagger}=r\circ\kappa_{1}

and similarly for
\kappa_{2}
. So there is a phase
U
on
C\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}C
with
q=r\circ U
. Then
m=p\circ u
where

u=i^{\dagger}\circ U\circ i

(19)

One may verify from the definitions of
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{B}
that any such endomorphism
u
preserves them, establishing the uniqueness property. Moreover, running the above argument with
p=\mathrm{id}
shows that any phase on
\mathrm{Im}(f)
is of the form (
19
). In particular, since phases on
C\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}C
are closed under the dagger, so are those on
\mathrm{Im}(f)
.
∎

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\mathbf{B}
be a dagger category with a phased dagger biproduct
A\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}B
. Then
\kappa_{A}
and
\kappa_{B}
are dagger kernels with
\kappa_{A}={\kappa_{B}}^{\bot}
.

Proof.

By definition both morphisms are isometries. Let
\pi_{A}:=\kappa_{A}^{\dagger}
and
\pi_{B}:=\kappa_{B}^{\dagger}
, and let us show that
\kappa_{A}=\ker(\pi_{B})
. Suppose that
f\colon C\to A\mathbin{\dot{\oplus}}B
has
\pi_{B}\circ f=0
, and let
g=\kappa_{A}\circ\pi_{A}\circ f
. Then
\pi_{A}\circ g=\pi_{A}\circ f
and
\pi_{B}\circ f=0=\pi_{B}\circ f
. Hence for some phase
U
we have

f=U\circ g=U\circ\kappa_{A}\circ\pi_{A}\circ f=\kappa_{A}\circ\pi_{A}\circ f

and so
f
factors over
\kappa_{A}
, as required.
∎

Proof of Example
7.7
.

We’ve seen that
\mathsf{Mat}_{S}
is always dagger compact with dagger biproducts.

First let us establish dagger normalisation.
For any state
\psi=(a_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\colon 1\to n
, since
S
is a phased ring we have

\psi^{\dagger}\circ\psi=\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot a_{i}=a^{\dagger}\cdot a

for some
a\in S
. Then if
\psi\neq 0
also
a\neq 0
and so
\phi=(\frac{a_{i}}{a})^{n}_{i=1}
is an isometry with
\psi=\phi\circ a
. We now show that
\mathsf{Mat}_{S}
has dagger kernels. Note that the states on any object
n\in\mathbb{N}
form the vector space
S^{n}
and also come with the ‘inner product’

\langle\psi,\phi\rangle:=\psi^{\dagger}\circ\phi

for
\psi,\phi\colon 1\to n
. Since
S
is a phased ring this satisfies
\langle\psi,\psi\rangle=0\implies\psi=0
.

Now for any morphism
M\colon n\to m
, the set
\{\psi\mid M\circ\psi=0\}
is a subspace of
S^{n}
and so has a finite basis
\{\psi_{i}\}^{r}_{i=1}
for some
r\leq n
. Using the well-known Gram-Schmidt algorithm (see e.g.
[
CK09
, p.544]
) we may replace this by another basis
\{\phi_{i}\}^{r}_{i=1}
which is orthonormal in that
\langle\phi_{j}^{\dagger},\phi_{i}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}
. Then
k:=(\phi_{i})^{r}_{i=1}\colon r\to n
in
\mathsf{Mat}_{S}
is an isometry with
k=\ker(M)
.

Next we verify homogeneity. Let
M,N\colon n\to m
satisfy
M^{\dagger}\circ M=N^{\dagger}\circ N
. It follows that
\mathrm{coim}(M)=\mathrm{coim}(N)
and so after restricting along these we may assume that
\ker(M)=\ker(N)=0
. Now define a modified ‘inner product’ by

\langle\psi,\phi\rangle^{\prime}:=\langle M\circ\psi,M\circ\phi\rangle=\langle N\circ\psi,N\circ\phi\rangle

Again this satisfies
\langle\psi,\psi\rangle^{\prime}=0\implies\psi=0
. Hence we may again apply the Gram-Schmidt algorithm to find an orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}\}^{n}_{i=1}
with respect to
\langle-,-\rangle^{\prime}
. Then
\{M\circ e_{i}\}^{n}_{i=1}
and
\{N\circ e_{i}\}^{n}_{i=1}
are each orthonormal collections of states of
m
and so may be extended to orthonormal bases
\{\psi_{i}\}^{m}_{i=1}
and
\{\phi_{i}\}^{m}_{i=1}
respectively. Finally, any matrix
U\colon m\to m
with
U\circ\psi_{i}=\phi_{i}
for all
i
is unitary and satisfies
U\circ M=N
.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a non-trivial quantum category. Then
\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
forms a dagger theory satisfying the operational principles. Moreover there is a dagger monoidal equivalence

\mathbf{A}\simeq\mathsf{GP}({\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})_{\mathsf{pure}}})

(20)

Proof.

By homogeneity in
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
has essentially unique dilations with respect to its environment structure
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
, i.e. the image of the functor
\widehat{(-)}\colon\mathbf{A}\to\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
. Now since
\mathbf{A}
has dagger kernels, it satisfies

f^{\dagger}\circ f=0\implies f=0

(21)

for all morphisms
f
, by
[
Vic11
, Lemma 2.4]
.
It follows that
\widehat{f}=0\implies f=0
in
\mathbf{A}
and

Proof of Lemma
9.2
.

Clearly
\mathbb{R}^{+}
satisfies these properties. Conversely if they hold then, by Lemmas
8.2
and
E.2
,

is a totally ordered Archimedean field
[
Hal11
]
with
R
as its positive elements. Let
(x_{n})^{\infty}_{n=1}
be any bounded monotonic sequence in
D(R)
. Then for some
r\in R
and
t=\pm 1
, the bounded sequence
(tx_{n}+r)^{\infty}_{n=1}
is increasing and belongs to
R
, and so converges there. Hence
(x_{n})^{\infty}_{n=1}
also converges in
D(R)
, making the latter monotone complete. But then by
[
Hal11
, Theorem 3.11]
there is an isomorphism
D(R)\simeq\mathbb{R}
and hence
R\simeq\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
∎

Appendix B
Notions of Purity

There have been several proposed notions of ‘pure’ morphism in the recent literature. The definition used here (
4
) from
[
Chi14
]
coincides with that of
[
SC17
]
whenever the ‘no leaks’ condition (
6
) is satisfied. In fact it is automatic in any theory with a suitable form of essentially unique purification, in the following sense.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a dagger theory with a zero object. Suppose that
\mathbf{C}
has an environment structure
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{p}}
which contains all zero morphisms and is state-inhabited, and for which dilations are essentially unique in the sense of (
5
). Suppose further that whenever
f\otimes\rho\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{p}}
for some causal state
\rho
, so does
f
. Then a morphism belongs to
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{p}}
iff it is pure.

Proof.

Let
f\colon A\to B
be non-zero and belong to
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{p}}
. Suppose that
f
has a dilation
g\colon A\to B\otimes C
. Dilating
g
if necessary, we may assume that
g\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{p}}
. Since
f
is non-zero,
C
is not a zero object and so has a causal state
\psi\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{p}}
. Then

g
f
=
\psi

and so

g
f
=
\psi
U

for some unitary
U\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{p}}
. By the CP axiom
U
is causal and hence so is the state
U\circ\psi
as desired. Conversely, suppose that
f\colon A\to B
is pure and non-zero. Let
g\colon A\to B\otimes C
be a dilation of
f
with
g\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{p}}
. Then we have

f
=
\sigma
A
B
C
g
A
C
B

for some causal state
\sigma
of
C
. Hence by assumption
f\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{p}}
.
∎

A more typical notion of purity for probabilistic theories instead refers to a process having no non-trivial decompositions as a mixture. Cunningham and Heunen have also presented an alternative, categorically well-behaved notion of purity
[
CH18
]
. Thanks to the presence of coarse-graining
+
, all of these notions may be defined in theories satisfying our principles, and in fact they coincide.

Appendix C
Equivalent Axioms

We have seen that the operational principles imply the presence of a coarse-graining operation
+
on processes. Taking this operation instead as primitive allows us to reformulate our principles in an alternative, operationally motivated manner. First let us note that this operation is surprisingly well-behaved.

Proposition C.1
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a dagger theory satisfying the operational principles. Then the following hold for all morphisms
f,g,h
:

•

f+g=f+h\implies g=h
;

•

f\otimes g=f\otimes h\implies f=0\text{ or }g=h
.

Proof.

We have
\mathbf{C}\simeq\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
for a quantum category
\mathbf{A}
. But the definition of coarse-graining in
\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})
is simply addition in
\mathbf{A}
. Since
\mathbf{A}
has negatives
-f
for all morphisms
f
it satisfies both properties.
∎

Under even milder assumptions we can deduce another property of coarse-graining.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a non-trivial compact dagger theory with coarse-graining and dagger kernels, satisfying strong purification and pure exclusion. Then all kernels are causal and
f+g=0\implies f=g=0
for all morphisms
f,g\colon A\to B
.

Proof.

Noting that causal complementation of kernels was not required for its proof, by Proposition
5.2
all kernels are pure isometries and hence causal by the CP axiom, and any non-trivial object
C
has an orthonormal pair of pure states
|0\rangle,|1\rangle
.

Now suppose that
f,g\colon A\to B
satisfy
f+g=0
. Then

Next, pure exclusion can also be deduced easily.

Lemma C.4

We can now present our principles in the equivalent manner of Theorem

.

Proof of Theorem
10.1
.

If
\mathbf{C}
satisfies the principles then the first point holds by Corollary

. If

\smash{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hskip-3.0pt

$\begin{aligned} \hbox to8.15pt{\vbox to8.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.07495pt\lower-3.75659pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{0.59999}{-0.525}{0.0}{0.0pt}{2.27626pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

for all effects
e
. But if this equation is satisfied then we get that
e\circ\rho=0
for any causal state
\rho
. In particular, since kernels are causal, for any causal pure state
\psi
of
\mathrm{Coim}(e)
we have
e\circ\mathrm{coim}(e)^{\dagger}\circ\psi=0
and so
\psi=0
. Hence
\mathrm{Coim}(e)=0
and so
e=0
.
∎

Appendix D
Pre-Quantum Categories

For any theory
\mathbf{C}
satisfying the operational principles,
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{pure}}
may be axiomatised as follows.

Definition D.1
.

A
pre-quantum
category
\mathbf{B}
is a dagger compact category with the superposition properties and dagger kernels, satisfying dagger normalisation and homogeneity as in Definition
7.1
, and for which
\mathrm{id}_{I}
is its only unitary scalar. We again call a pre-quantum category
non-trivial
when
\mathrm{id}_{I}\neq 0
.

In fact pre-quantum categories can be equivalently defined under a weakening of (
2
) called ‘positive-freeness’; see
[
Tul18a
, Chapter 6]
.

Proposition D.2
.

The correspondence of Theorem
7.9
extends to include non-trivial pre-quantum categories
\mathbf{B}
, via
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{pure}}
and
\mathbf{A}=\mathsf{GP}({\mathbf{B}})
.

Proof.

Firstly, if
\mathbf{C}
satisfies the operational principles, we must check that
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{pure}}
is a pre-quantum category. But this is immediate from the results of Sections
5
and
6
and strong purification.

Next, the proof that if
\mathbf{B}
is a pre-quantum category then
\mathbf{A}:=\mathsf{GP}({\mathbf{B}})
is a quantum category is almost exactly that of Proposition
7.2
(replacing
\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{pure}}
by
\mathbf{B}
). Observe also that if
\mathbf{B}
is non-trivial then so is
\mathbf{A}
. Finally, for the correspondence, note that in this case
\mathbf{B}\simeq\mathbf{A}_{\sim}\simeq\mathsf{CPM}(\mathbf{A})_{\mathsf{pure}}
, with the latter equivalence found in the proof of Theorem
7.9
.
∎

Appendix E
Phased Rings and Fields

We have left open the question of whether every phased ring
S
is in fact a field. Two results in this direction are as follows.

Lemma E.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a quantum category,
S
its ring of scalars and
R=S^{\mathrm{pos}}
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

R
is a semi-field;

2.

S
is a field;

3.

In
\mathbf{A}
the only sub-objects of
I
are
\{0,I\}
.

Proof.

1

\iff

2
:
R\subseteq S
and an element
s\in S
is invertible iff
s^{\dagger}\cdot s
is.

2

\implies

3
:
Let
m\colon A\rightarrowtail I
be monic. Then if
r:=m\circ m^{\dagger}
is zero then
m=0
by (
21
). Otherwise
r
is invertible and hence so is
m
.

3

\implies

2
:
Thanks to Lemma
5.1
every non-zero scalar
r
has
\ker(r)=0
. Since
\mathbf{A}
has negatives by Proposition
7.4
, this makes
r
monic and hence an isomorphism by assumption.
∎

Recall the order
\leq
on
S^{\mathrm{pos}}
from Lemma
8.2
.

Lemma E.2
.

Let
S
be a phased ring and suppose that every
a\in S^{\mathrm{pos}}
has
\frac{1}{n}\leq a
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
S
is a field.

Proof.

From the definition of a phased ring, we have that for
a,b\in S^{\mathrm{pos}}
whenever
a\leq b
then
a
is divisible by
b
. If
a\neq 0
then
1\leq a\cdot n
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, making
a\cdot n
invertible and hence
a
also. Then every
s\in S
is invertible since
s^{\dagger}\cdot s\in S^{\mathrm{pos}}
is.
∎

Appendix F
Hilbert Categories

Our notion of a quantum category is similar to the following axiomatization of the category
\mathbf{Hilb}
due to Chris Heunen
[
Heu09
]
. A
Hilbert category
is a dagger symmetric monoidal category
\mathbf{H}
with dagger equalisers and finite dagger biproducts such that:

•

every isometry is a kernel;

•

every morphism
f\colon A\to B
has a
bound
in that there is some scalar
s
such that for every
\psi\colon I\to A
we have
\psi^{\dagger}\circ f^{\dagger}\circ f\circ\psi=(s^{\dagger}\circ s)\circ(\psi^{\dagger}\circ\psi)+r
for some positive scalar
r
.

Lemma F.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a quantum category. Then
\mathbf{A}
is a well-pointed Hilbert category.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.4
it remains to verify that every morphism
f\colon A\to B
has a bound
s
as above. Thanks to dagger normalisation it suffices to consider when
\psi
is an isometry and hence a kernel. Then letting
c=\mathrm{coker}(\psi)
by Proposition
7.4
(
4
) we have
\mathrm{id}_{A}=\psi\circ\psi^{\dagger}+c^{\dagger}\circ c
so that:

f
f
=
f
f
\psi
\psi
+
f
f
c
c

since the right-hand scalar is positive, we may take
s
as the left-hand scalar.
∎

By
[
Heu09
, Theorem 4]
this means that whenever
\mathbf{A}
is a quantum category which is locally small and has that its ring of scalars
S
is a field (as in Lemma
E.1
) of at most continuum cardinality, there is a lax dagger monoidal embedding
\mathbf{A}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Hilb}
up to some isomorphism of
S
. It would be interesting to further explore the connections between our result and Heunen’s.
